# Psalms brief

Book-level decisions for Psalms. The site-wide standard is in `STANDARDS.md`; this file records what is particular to Psalms. Writers, checkers, and reviewers read both before starting a psalm. Jeremiah's brief (`content/jeremiah/BRIEF.md`) is the model, and its section “What a chapter is for” holds here.

**Status (2026-10-10):** drafted by the coordinator from the sources named below, each read in the session with the research scripts. The author chose the book (“Let's do the book of Psalms next”) and **has not read this brief**. Where a section says “Proposed,” that is the approach the chapters will follow; it is the coordinator's decision, not a fact from a source, and the author can overturn it. **The first batch is twelve psalms chosen to try this brief** (author, 2026-10-10: “let's try with a batch of 12 and see how far we get”): 1, 2, 3, 8, 22, 23, 24, 51, 82, 110, 137, 150. What follows it is the author's to decide once it has been measured and read. 

Related files: `OPEN-QUESTIONS.md` at the top of the repository (what waits for the author, for every book); `THEME-CANDIDATES.md` in this folder.

## State of the book

`node scripts/status.mjs psalms` prints what the repository shows; the history is `git log -- content/psalms`. What a script can't see:

- **The book is set up in preview** (2026-10-10): `book.yaml` with the five books as divisions, the text imported and matched to the Gospel Library edition (“The text,” below), and the Church's footnotes for every psalm saved in `.cache/batch/psalms/fn<n>.txt`.
- **Twelve psalms are written and reviewed** (2026-10-10: 1, 2, 3, 8, 22, 23, 24, 51, 82, 110, 137, 150), every reviewer reporting its psalm ready. They have had the language pass (2026-10-10; Codex's side-by-side reading left the “Blessed” frame of Psalms 1 and 2 with Psalm 1, and the explanation of titles with Psalm 3). The author has read none. Of their 115 notes, Delitzsch is cited in 29, a Latter-day Saint article or KnoWhy in 51, and the Student Manual in 2 (`OPEN-QUESTIONS.md`, Ps13).
- **Where the run stands (2026-10-10, evening).** The author said to run the rest in batches; the batches after the first are 4–16, 17–31, 32–43, 44–56, 57–68, 69–80, 81–93, 94–105, 106–118, 119–130, 131–143 and 144–149 (the last with `lastBatch: true`). `node scripts/status.mjs psalms` shows which psalms are written and which have had the language pass. When this was written, Psalms 1–47, 49–54 and 56 were finished through every step; Psalms 48 and 55 were being written again (48's writer wrote nothing, and 55 was committed without its review, because both agents took a question the author asked mid-run for their task); and Psalms 57–68 were being written. The author's usage limit stops a batch in flight: afterwards, run `write-chapters` again for the psalms with no committed chapter (a draft left behind is picked up), then `batch-finish.mjs`, then `codex-pass`, then `batch-commit.mjs … pass`. After a batch, look for a chapter whose writer or reviewer did nothing: `batch-finish` prints NOT READY for one, and a psalm that `check-pass` calls unchanged had no Codex reading.
- **What a psalm costs.** The first batch took 36 minutes and 5.2 million tokens for twelve psalms through writer, checker and reviewer, about 430,000 a psalm, which is what a chapter of any other book has cost: a short psalm is not cheaper, because most of the cost is the research and the brief, not the verses. With the language pass (about 120,000 a chapter) the remaining 138 psalms would be roughly 75 million tokens in twelve batches.
- **This brief's research began with Scripture Central, the Religious Studies Center, BYU Studies and the Interpreter Foundation** (author, 2026-10-09); the Student Manual was opened last and Delitzsch not at all beyond seeing that his pages load. What was searched and what was read is under “Sources for Psalms.”

## What the author has decided

Nothing for Psalms in particular yet. The author's standing rules are in `STANDARDS.md` §1 and `AUTHORING.md` and are not repeated here. How three of them land on this book:

- **Newest trumps oldest.** The newest is *Scripture Helps: Old Testament* (2025), three chapters that between them treat about seventy psalms. The Student Manual's one chapter on the whole book is older, and it defends David's authorship of the Psalter against “modern critics” in words *Scripture Helps* no longer uses (“attributed to,” “credited with”). Follow *Scripture Helps*.
- **Don't replicate the Gospel Library.** *Scripture Helps* prints a table of eight psalm verses beside their New Testament fulfilments, and answers some two dozen questions verse by verse (selah, the sons of Korah, Asaph, “ye are gods,” the two Lords of Psalm 110, “perfect hatred”). A note does not repeat one of those answers; it goes where the help stops.
- **Quote the Church's material sparingly.** President Nelson on *hesed* is printed in *Scripture Helps* at Psalm 50:5 and will tempt every psalm that has “lovingkindness” or “mercy.” It is quoted once on the site, at the first psalm where the word carries the note (Proposed: 51:1, or 136 if the author would rather), from the talk's own page; elsewhere, link.

## What a chapter is for here

A psalm is not a chapter of a longer argument. Each is a whole poem with its own speaker, occasion, and shape, and most are short: a reader can hold all of Psalm 23 in mind at once. So the reader's questions are a poem's questions. *Who is speaking, and to whom? What has happened to him? Where does the psalm turn, and why? What is this image? Why does he ask God to break his enemies' teeth? Did David write it? Is this about Christ, and how do we know?*

**The thread is the psalm's movement.** Most psalms turn: from complaint to trust, from a call to praise to the reasons for it, from the king's danger to his victory. The `thread` says where the turn is and what makes it. A lament that ends in praise without saying what changed (Psalm 13, Psalm 22 at verse 21) is the question, not a problem to smooth over.

**Say what the psalm says of its own speaker before what it foretells.** Psalm 22 is first the prayer of a man surrounded and wasting, and then the psalm the Savior prayed on the cross and the evangelists saw fulfilled in detail. Give the prayer, then the fulfilment, and keep them apart (“Messianic psalms,” below).

**Shared things are explained once.** A hundred and fifty poems share a small stock of words: selah, the title terms, *hesed*, Sheol, Zion and the holy hill, the horn, the right hand, the wicked and the righteous, the anointed. Each is explained once, at the place this brief names, and every other psalm points there with a `[[reference]]`. This matters more here than in any book so far: without it the same note will be written forty times. `check-content psalms --repeats` after each batch shows what crept in.

**Short psalms get short chapters.** Psalm 117 has two verses, 131 and 133 and 134 three each. `setting` may fall under the checker's 120 words for these, and the warning is ignored; two or three notes is a full chapter. Don't pad. Psalm 119 (176 verses) is the other extreme: one section to a stanza of eight verses, and notes on what a reader asks (the eight words for the law, the acrostic, the lines everyone knows), not on every stanza.

## The first batch

Psalms 1, 2, 3, 8, 22, 23, 24, 51, 82, 110, 137 and 150, written out of order and at the same time, so no writer can see another's chapter. Where this brief says a thing is explained “once, at its first appearance,” in this batch that means:

| Explained in full at | What | The others in the batch |
|---|---|---|
| Psalm 1 | how Hebrew verse works (the second half of a line answering the first); that Psalms 1 and 2 open the collection; when the book was gathered | point to `[[Ps. 1:1]]` |
| Psalm 2 | “his anointed” (the word behind Messiah) and what a royal psalm is | 110 points to `[[Ps. 2:2]]` |
| Psalm 3 | selah (3:2); what a title is and what it is worth, in a note on the title | say nothing of selah; a title's occasion in a sentence |
| Psalm 8 | “To the chief Musician” and Gittith, in a note on the title; *Elohim* in 8:5 | 82 points to `[[Ps. 8:5]]` for the word and gives its own council |
| Psalm 22 | the shape of a lament (address, complaint, plea, vow of praise) | 3, 51 and 137 use the word “lament” without re-explaining |
| Psalm 24 | ascending the hill of the Lord; the temple in the Psalms | 23:6 and 150:1 point to `[[Ps. 24:3]]` |
| Psalm 51 | David after Nathan; *hesed* (51:1), with President Nelson quoted once | 23:6 points to `[[Ps. 51:1]]` |
| Psalm 110 | LORD and Lord (110:1); Melchizedek | 2 and 8 point to `[[Ps. 110:1]]` |
| Psalm 137 | the cursing psalms | 3:7 and 110:5–6 point to `[[Ps. 137:8]]` in a clause if the reader would ask |
| Psalm 150 | hallelujah; the five books and their closing lines of praise | Psalm 1 says only that there are five |

A pointer is written to the verse named here even though that psalm's note may end up on a neighboring verse; the coordinator mends pointers after the batch.

## Hebrew: only to answer a reader's question

(Author, 2026-10-11: “Understanding the Hebrew can be helpful if it sheds insight into English text in answering the questions that come up. You don't need to make an index of the hebrew unless you really need it.”) In the first 96 psalms, 48 percent of the notes are Language notes and 79 percent cite the Hebrew text or the lexicon; Jeremiah has 21 and 15. **From Psalm 94 on** (writers, checkers and reviewers alike; `STANDARDS.md` §3 has the rule in full):

- Start from the English verse and the reader's question. Bring in a Hebrew word only when it answers that question: the King James word is obscure or misleading, a disputed rendering changes the reading, or the passage turns on one word the English splits in two.
- Don't say what Hebrew word lies behind an ordinary English one, count a word's occurrences, list where a root returns, or remark that two lines share a word, unless the reading changes because of it. That goes for `thread` and `setting` too.
- No more than about a quarter of a psalm's notes are Language notes, and a psalm may have none. A note that would be only a word study is cut, not rescued with another source.
- Where no Latter-day Saint source speaks to a psalm, the answer is a shorter chapter resting on the text, the scriptures that take it up, and what the Church's helps say, not a chapter filled with the lexicon.
- A reviewer of a psalm written before this rule (Psalms 94–105 were being written when it was given) applies it: cut Hebrew that answers no question a reader of the English would ask, with its ledger rows.

Where this brief elsewhere sends a writer to `hebrew.mjs` and a lexicon page (the title terms, *hesed*, Sheol, the names of God, a shared English word), that is where to look when the question is real, not a list of notes to write. The first 93 psalms are not gone over (`AUTHORING.md`: rules are for new writing) unless the author asks.

## The rest of the book

(Author, 2026-10-10: “Run the rest of the Psalms, in batches.”) The other 138 psalms are written in order, twelve at a time. For a writer:

- **Twelve psalms are already written: 1, 2, 3, 8, 22, 23, 24, 51, 82, 110, 137, 150.** What the table above says they explain, they explain; point there and don't explain it again. Psalms 8 and 22 are the models for a chapter and for a note on a title.
- **Everything else that is “explained once” is explained at the psalm this brief names** (“Book-wide conventions” and “Contested and sensitive questions”: Neginoth at 4, Nehiloth at 5, Sheminith and Sheol at 6, Shiggaion at 7, Michtam at 16, Maschil at 32, the sons of Korah at 42, Alamoth at 46, Asaph at 50, the songs of degrees at 120, and so on). If your psalm is that psalm, write the full note. If it is not, point to that psalm's verse with a `[[reference]]` whether or not it is written yet: the psalms of a batch are written at the same time, and the coordinator mends pointers afterward.
- **A term this brief does not place** (a title word, an image) is explained at the lowest-numbered psalm that has it; `node scripts/occurrences.mjs psalms "<word>"` shows which that is. Title words are not in that search: for those, `grep -n "<word>" data/kjv/psalms.titles.json`.
- **The title is on the page** as verse 0. Don't quote it in `setting`; put what its words need in a note with `ref: 0`.

## Divisions

The Psalter's own five books, each closed by a line of praise (41:13; 72:19; 89:52; 106:48; Psalm 150 closing the whole), as *Scripture Helps* and the Student Manual (28-2) both give them. They are in `book.yaml` as Book One to Book Five (keys `psalms1` to `psalms5`):

| Psalms | Book | *Scripture Helps* says of it |
|---|---|---|
| 1–41 | One | Psalms 1–2 introduce the collection; most are attributed to David |
| 42–72 | Two | hope in the Messiah despite opposition; several of David's pleas for mercy |
| 73–89 | Three | God as judge; hope for the covenant's fulfilment |
| 90–106 | Four | opens with the prayer of Moses; the Lord's reign |
| 107–150 | Five | praise for deliverance; the Hallel (113–118), the songs of degrees (120–134), the closing hallelujahs (146–150) |

## Book-wide conventions

**What in this brief is a lead and not a fact.** Words in quotation marks below were copied from the page named. The rest (what a Hebrew word can mean, which psalms share lines, how a modern translation prints a verse, which later scripture takes up which line) is the coordinator's direction of where to look, some of it from memory. None of it goes into a chapter until the writer has read it on a page and given it a ledger row (`STANDARDS.md` §4).

- **The titles.** Most psalms have a title above verse 1 (“A Psalm of David, when he fled from Absalom his son”). In the Hebrew it is part of the text, usually verse 1, so **Hebrew verse numbers in those psalms run one ahead of the King James** (two ahead in a few); `scripts/hebrew.mjs psalms 3:1` prints the title's words first and gives both numbers. Commentaries may follow the Hebrew; cite the King James verse. **The site shows the title above verse 1 as verse 0** (author, 2026-10-10: “just go do it”): `node scripts/show.mjs psalms <n>` and `footnotes.mjs` print it, a note with `ref: 0` hangs on its words, and its ledger rows say `note <psalm>:0 “…”` (`STANDARDS.md` §6). So the page already prints the title: `setting` does not quote it, and what a reader would ask of its words (a term, a tune name, the occasion) is a note on the title, not a paragraph of `setting`. Psalms 3, 8 and 22 show how. A title that says only “A Psalm of David” needs no note.
- **What a title is worth.** The Bible Dictionary: “it is open to question whether these are as old as the words to which they are attached,” and “it has been concluded that the Hebrew titles are sometimes inaccurate.” *Scripture Helps* says psalms are “attributed to” David and that Asaph “is credited with” his. The New Testament and the Savior quote particular psalms as David's (Psalm 110 in `[[Matt. 22:43]]`; Psalm 16 in `[[Acts 2:25]]`; Psalm 2 in `[[Acts 4:25]]`). **Proposed:** a chapter says “the title ascribes it to David” or “a psalm of David by its title” and takes the occasion a title gives (Absalom, Doeg, the cave, Bathsheba) as the setting the psalm asks to be read in, with the narrative chapter cited from 1 or 2 Samuel. Where the New Testament names David, the chapter says David without hedging. No chapter argues for or against a title, and none dates a psalm by its language or theology except from a source read, as that source's.
- **`when`.** Proposed: from the title where there is one (“By its title, David's, when he fled from Absalom”); “Undated” otherwise, with the little the psalm itself shows (137 is by the rivers of Babylon; 126 looks back on the return). KnoWhy #867 and *Scripture Helps* both say the book was gathered over a long time and likely put together after the exile; that is said once, on the book page and in Psalm 1's setting.
- **The title terms.** The Bible Dictionary's entry “Psalms” explains them in one paragraph (Neginoth, Nehiloth, Alamoth, Sheminith, Maschil, Michtam, Shiggaion, Gittith, the tune names); the Student Manual (28-4) has a longer list. **There is no Bible Dictionary entry “Maschil” or “Michtam”** (those addresses return the index); there are entries “Selah,” “Hallel,” “Degrees, songs of,” “Asaph,” “Korah,” “David,” “Music.” Each term is explained where it first appears, in a note on the title, and never again: “To the chief Musician” at Psalm 8 (written in the first batch; Psalm 4's title note has a clause and points there), Neginoth at Psalm 4, Nehiloth at 5, Sheminith at 6, Shiggaion at 7, Gittith at 8, Michtam at 16, Maschil at 32, the sons of Korah at 42, Alamoth at 46, Asaph at 50, the songs of degrees at 120. Most are uncertain, and the chapter says so in the Bible Dictionary's own hedges (“seem to imply,” “may refer”).
- **Selah.** Explained once, at 3:2, its first use: the meaning is uncertain (*Scripture Helps*, at Psalm 3:2; the Bible Dictionary, “Selah”; Hopkin and Hopkin say nearly all scholars take it as an instruction to the performers). No later psalm has a note on it.
- **Numbering in the Greek.** The Septuagint counts most psalms one lower than the Hebrew and English (Psalm 23 is its 22). Brenton's translation on Bible Hub is arranged by the English number with the Greek in parentheses (`biblehub.com/sep/psalms/23.htm` opens “1(22:1)”). When citing the Septuagint, give the English number and say “the Greek” without the other number unless a source cited uses it.
- **Kinds of psalm.** Scholars sort the psalms by form: laments of one person or of the people, hymns of praise, thanksgivings, royal psalms, wisdom psalms. KnoWhy #438 and Larsen's *Old Testament Minute* use the terms. They help a reader see why a psalm is built as it is (a lament's address, complaint, plea, and vow of praise), so `thread` may use one, defined in a clause. Don't open a chapter by classifying it, and don't say “scholars classify.”
- **Poetry.** Hebrew verse says a thing twice: the second half of a line echoes, sharpens, or answers the first. That is explained once, at Psalm 1 or 2 (`ot-manual-g`, the Student Manual's enrichment section on Hebrew literary styles, is in `sources.yaml`; look first for a BYU or Scripture Central treatment). Afterward a note points out parallelism only where it settles what a line means (Psalm 8:4's “man” and “son of man”; 22:16–20), and a `structure` note is written only when the shape is the message (`STANDARDS.md` §7). Acrostics (a lead: Psalms 9–10, 25, 34, 37, 111, 112, 119, 145) are said in a sentence in `setting`; *Scripture Helps* explains 119's.
- **Names of God.** “LORD” in capitals is the divine name; “Lord” is *adonai*; “God” is *Elohim* or *El*. Psalm 110:1 has the first two side by side, and *Scripture Helps* explains them there. Copy any phrase with a name from `data/kjv/psalms.json`: the edition's capitals were checked verse by verse. Books Two and Three use “God” far more than “LORD” (a lead: Psalm 14 and Psalm 53 are one psalm with the names changed); say it where a reader would notice, from a source read.
- **Psalms that repeat.** Leads, each to be confirmed in the text before it is said: Psalm 18 is David's song in 2 Samuel 22; 14 and 53 are nearly one psalm; 70 is 40:13–17; 108 joins the ends of 57 and 60; parts of 96, 105 and 106 are sung in 1 Chronicles 16; 9 and 10, and 42 and 43, may each be one poem. The second chapter says so in a sentence, links to the first, and notes only what differs.
- **Other books on the site.** Isaiah, Jeremiah, Lamentations, Ezekiel, Daniel, Hosea, Joel, Malachi and Genesis are written. Link where a psalm shares a line or an image with one of them (the shepherd of Psalm 23 and `[[Ezek. 34:11]]`; Psalm 137 and Lamentations; Leviathan in 74 and 104 and `[[Isa. 27:1]]`; “The LORD reigneth” and Isaiah 52:7), and don't say who borrowed from whom except as a source read says.
- **The Book of Mormon.** Nephi and Jacob use the Psalms more than anyone else in the book: Hilton counts the allusions and finds sixty-three percent in those two; Scripture Central has KnoWhys on Jacob's use of Psalm 95 (#62), Nephi's of Psalm 24 (#51), Lehi's of Psalm 51 (#325), Zenos's of Psalm 46 (#313), laments (#438), temple psalms (#437), and “Nephi's psalm” (#30). A note may say that a Nephite prophet takes up the line, from the KnoWhy or Hilton, where it shows how the psalm was read. **No comparison panel**, and nothing is written for a Book of Mormon chapter (`AUTHORING.md`: no more Book of Mormon content for now).
- **Joseph Smith Translation.** Joseph Fielding McConkie's essay counts changes in nearly two hundred verses of Psalms, most of them small, and almost two dozen major ones, more than in any other book of the Writings; David LeFevre's chapter dates the work to early 1833 and reads the changes beside the Prophet's own circumstances. The Church's edition carries few of them: the saved footnotes show a Joseph Smith Translation reading in eleven psalms only (11, 14, 15, 19, 24, 30, 32, 50, 109, 138, 141). A change that is not in the footnotes (Psalm 46's, which *Scripture Helps* quotes in part) is used only from a page that prints it: *Scripture Helps*, McConkie, or LeFevre. Use a change where it answers a reader's question (Psalm 14:1, where the fool's saying becomes a denial that anyone has seen God, has a BYU Studies article); a change the footnote already shows is not a note by itself.
- **Hymns.** Many hymns come from psalms, and the hymnbook has a scripture index. That is reception history (`STANDARDS.md` §1, “Not a compendium”): leave it out, except a line in `explore` where a psalm is sung by the Saints nearly as written (Psalm 23; Psalm 100).
- **Theme candidates.** A writer reports what runs across the book; the coordinator adds it to `THEME-CANDIDATES.md`. No theme page is written until the author chooses it.

**Habits to avoid** (Ezekiel's brief has the examples): say a thing once in a chapter; don't re-explain what another chapter explains, point with a `[[reference]]`; a Witness of Christ does not open by saying what the psalm lacks; a note's first sentence gives the answer, since the site shows it alone as the note's card; a reading stays a reading in every sentence; “In plain words” adds nothing to the verse, and with poetry the risk is the opposite one too: don't flatten an image into its moral; Hebrew only where it changes what the reader understands; don't join two verses yourself and call it the text's meaning; no personal application, which in the Psalms means no “when we feel as David felt.”

## Contested and sensitive questions

One approach for each, so that a hundred and fifty chapters say the same thing. Each quotation was copied from the page named, read with `source.mjs` on 2026-10-10.

- **Messianic psalms: prophecy, or prayer later fulfilled?** Hopkin and Hopkin put the two views plainly: that an inspired David “foretold precisely and clearly of Jesus Christ,” and that the psalms were written “to reflect the yearnings, experiences, and understanding of Israelites in their time” and were seen afterward to fit the Savior's life; and they answer, “For Latter-day Saints, these two viewpoints need not be seen as mutually exclusive.” *Scripture Helps*: such psalms “prophetically refer to the life and mission of Jesus Christ.” The Bible Dictionary: “No book of the Old Testament is more Christian in its inner sense.” **Proposed:** the chapter gives the psalm as its speaker's prayer or the king's song first, then what the New Testament does with it, quoting the New Testament verse; where the Savior or an apostle says the psalm spoke of Him (Psalm 110, Psalm 16, Psalm 22, Psalm 118:22, Psalm 2), the chapter says so as scripture's own reading and does not hedge it. A psalm no scripture or Church source reads of Christ gets a short `christ` section resting on what the psalm says of the Lord, who is Jehovah, and no typology of the writer's own. The psalms the endnotes of *Scripture Helps* call royal (2, 18, 45, 72, 89, 110, 132) are about Israel's king first; say what was asked of and promised to the king, then where the New Testament carries it.
- **Psalm 22.** Shon Hopkin's article in *BYU Studies* is the Latter-day Saint study, and it calls Psalm 22 and Isaiah 53 “the twin pillars of Old Testament prophecy regarding Christ.” Isaiah 53 is on the site: link. **“They pierced my hands and my feet” (22:16)**: the Hebrew text as printed reads differently (“like a lion”), the Septuagint has “pierced,” and a Dead Sea scroll bears on it; KnoWhy #641 and Hopkin's earlier article (“The Psalm 22:16 Controversy,” *BYU Studies* 44, no. 3) lay it out. Give the evidence in the order text, Greek, scroll, and the conclusion as theirs. What the Savior's cry of verse 1 meant (whether the Father withdrew) has been taught by apostles; quote one, from the talk's own page, and don't adjudicate beyond it.
- **David.** The psalms of David's trouble are read beside his story, including its worst part. Psalm 51's title places it after Nathan came to him. *Scripture Helps* there: David “could be forgiven of his sins, except for the murder of Uriah.” `[[D&C 132:39]]` says he “hath fallen from his exaltation,” and Peter that his soul was not left in hell reads Psalm 16 of Christ and of David's own hope (`[[Acts 2:27–31]]`; *Scripture Helps* at 16:10). The chapter on 51 says this once, from those sources, and the other penitential psalms link to it. Elsewhere David is the psalmist the titles and the New Testament present, and a chapter does not keep reminding the reader of Bathsheba.
- **The cursing psalms** (a lead: 35, 58, 69, 83, 109, 137:8–9, 139:19–22, and lines in many others). A reader stops at every one. *Scripture Helps* answers at 139:19–22 in three sentences: the words “may have been used to express unwavering loyalty to God,” and “we should keep in mind the higher law taught by Jesus Christ,” quoting the Sermon on the Mount. The Student Manual (28-5) prints a longer answer from a handbook: the psalmist is a plaintiff before a judge, and “Vengeance is always seen as God's province, and his alone.” **Proposed:** one full note, at the first psalm where a curse is the question (Psalm 5:10 or 35, whichever is written first in the batch plan; later ones point to it), saying three things from sources read: the psalmist asks God to act and does not act himself; the prayer is against those who attack the helpless or God's people, in the terms of the covenant's own curses; and the Savior's command to love enemies is the higher law. Then each later psalm notes only what is its own (who the enemy is; that Peter applies 69:25 and 109:8 to Judas in `[[Acts 1:20]]`; that 137's last verse answers what Babylon did, described and not quoted). Never explain a curse away as “only poetry,” and never apply one.
- **“Ye are gods” (82:6) and the council of gods.** *Scripture Helps*: the psalm opens with God “standing among a group of heavenly beings. This setting, sometimes called the divine council, is described in several places throughout the Old Testament,” and yet “God appears to be addressing those He had appointed as judges but who had judged unjustly,” so that the phrase “likely refers to” their delegated authority; the Savior cites the verse in `[[John 10:34]]`; and “Modern prophets have referenced Psalm 82:6 when teaching that we are the children of God.” Latter-day Saint scholars have argued at length that the “gods” are heavenly beings (the FARMS Review exchange with Michael Heiser is on Scripture Central's archive; Daniel McClellan's article in *Interpreter* could not be opened). **Proposed:** follow *Scripture Helps*, which holds both the council and the judges; give the heavenly-beings reading as one that Latter-day Saint scholars have defended, from a page read; quote the Savior. The same care at 8:5, where *Scripture Helps* says “angels” translates *Elohim* and that “a little lower than God” is more accurate, and at 89:5–7, 95:3, 97:7 and 138:1.
- **Sheol and what the psalmist knew of life after death.** “In death there is no remembrance of thee” (6:5), “Shall the dust praise thee?” (30:9), “the dead praise not the LORD” (115:17), beside 16:10, 49:15 and 73:24. *Scripture Helps* (at 16:10): “hell” is *sheol*, “the place where all people go after death, regardless of their moral character,” and “generally viewed as inescapable.” Explain *sheol* once, at 6:5; say what each psalm says without correcting it from later revelation in the psalmist's mouth, and then what the Restoration adds (the spirit world: the Guide to the Scriptures or a talk read), as addition.
- **“In sin did my mother conceive me” (51:5).** *Scripture Helps* answers it with Brigham Young: not original sin. It sits beside the verse, so the chapter does not repeat it; if a note is written it is on what the Hebrew parallelism is doing in the line.
- **The temple in the Psalms.** The richest Latter-day Saint writing on the book: Skinner on seeing God in His temple; Hopkin and Hopkin on psalms sung by Levites during the ordinances; KnoWhy #437 (“temple” appears in Psalms more than in any Old Testament book but 2 Chronicles); Parry's proposal that “generation” in 24:6 could be read as a circle of worshippers; KnoWhy #642 and Matthew Brown on the king grasping God's hand (73:23); Larsen's *Old Testament Minute*, which reads Psalm 1's tree as the tree of life by the temple and the blessed man as the king of Psalm 2. Much of this is reconstruction: what was said or done at the gate, in a procession, at an enthronement. **Proposed:** what the psalm itself says about the house, the hill, the gates, the altar, and the face of God is stated plainly; a reconstruction of ritual is given as “one reading” or “has been proposed,” once, with its source in the note's `sources`; and no note draws a line from a psalm to the modern temple endowment. Psalm 24:3–4 is the anchor (*Scripture Helps* has a section; Elder Bednar's “Clean Hands and a Pure Heart” is in its Learn More): the full note on ascending the hill of the Lord is there, and 15, 26, 27, 43, 84, 118:19–20 and 120–134 point to it.
- **Psalm 110.** Two Lords (verse 1) and a priest for ever after the order of Melchizedek (verse 4); *Scripture Helps* treats both, with `[[Alma 13:1–19]]` and D&C 107 behind the second. The most quoted psalm in the New Testament (a lead; say it only from a source read). Melchizedek is on the site at `[[Gen. 14:18]]`: link. The note's own work is the Savior's question in `[[Matt. 22:41–46]]` and how Hebrews 5–7 argues from verse 4.
- **Creation and the sea monster** (74:13–14; 89:9–10; 104:26). KnoWhy #866 is on Leviathan and on creation told as the Lord's victory over the sea. Isaiah 27:1 and 51:9 on the site touch it; link, and give the KnoWhy's reading as its reading. These verses are poetry about the Creator's power, and no chapter sets them against Genesis 1.
- **Hesed.** “Lovingkindness” and “mercy” in the King James (Psalm 136 has it in every verse). *Scripture Helps* at 26:1–3, 50:5, 51:1 and 103:17–18 explains it as covenantal love, with President Nelson. One full note (see “Quote the Church's material sparingly,” above), with the Hebrew from `hebrew.mjs` and a lexicon page; `[[Hosea 6:4]]` on the site has a note on the word: read it first and agree with it.
- **Psalm 137:9.** Described, not quoted (`STANDARDS.md` §3). It is the cursing-psalms question at its hardest; see above, and `scripture-helps-violence` (the appendix on violence in the Old Testament, in `sources.yaml`).
- **Psalm 45, a royal wedding song** quoted of the Son in `[[Heb. 1:8–9]]`. “Thy throne, O God” is addressed to the king in the psalm; say what the line is in the psalm and then what Hebrews does. Whether it bears on the Savior's marriage is not raised.
- **Psalm 2:7, “this day have I begotten thee.”** KnoWhy #865 is on it (and #655 on the baptism). The king's adoption at his enthronement, and then `[[Acts 13:33]]` and `[[Heb. 1:5]]`. Don't build a doctrine of when the Son was begotten from the psalm.

## Sources for Psalms

Tested 2026-10-10 with the research scripts. `STANDARDS.md` §9 has the general tools.

**What was searched.** Scripture Central's KnoWhys (`knowhys.mjs "Psalm"`: 155 mention the book; those below are on it) and its archive by web search; the Interpreter Foundation by web search (its journal list returned a server error and its article pages no text, twice: see “Not readable”); `rsc.byu.edu` and `byustudies.byu.edu` by web search; BYU Studies' bibliography of Latter-day Saint writing on the Old Testament (`--find "Psalm"`). Then *Scripture Helps*, the Bible Dictionary and the Guide to the Scriptures; the Student Manual last.

**Latter-day Saint scholarship, readable** (propose an entry when you use one that has no key; the key to use is given):

- **The 2013 Sperry Symposium, *Ascending the Mountain of the Lord: Temple, Praise, and Worship in the Old Testament*** (RSC), all on `rsc.byu.edu/ascending-mountain-lord/…`:
  - Andrew C. Skinner, “Seeing God in His Temple: A Significant Theme in Israel's Psalms” (`…/seeing-god-his-temple-significant-theme-israels-psalms`; `skinner-seeing-god`).
  - J. Arden Hopkin and Shon D. Hopkin, “The Psalms Sung: The Power of Music in Sacred Worship,” 329–48 (`…/psalms-sung-power-music-sacred-worship`; `hopkin-psalms-sung`): the two views of messianic psalms, the Levites and the temple, selah, Psalm 15 as call and response.
  - John Hilton III, “Old Testament Psalms in the Book of Mormon” (`…/old-testament-psalms-book-mormon`; `hilton-psalms-bom`).
  - David A. LeFevre, “‘Give Me Right Word, O Lord’: The JST Changes in the Psalms” (`…/give-me-right-word-o-lord-jst-changes-psalms`; `lefevre-jst-psalms`).
  - Kenneth L. Alford and D. Bryce Baker, “Parallels between Psalms 25–31 and the Psalm of Nephi” (`…/parallels-between-psalms-25-31-psalm-nephi`).
- Shon D. Hopkin, “‘My God, My God, Why Hast Thou Forsaken Me?’: Psalm 22 and the Mission of Christ,” *BYU Studies* 52, no. 4 (2013): 117– (`byustudies.byu.edu/article/my-god-my-god-why-hast-thou-forsaken-me-psalm-22-and-the-mission-of-christ`; `hopkin-psalm-22`). His “The Psalm 22:16 Controversy: New Evidence from the Dead Sea Scrolls” is on Scripture Central's archive (`…/archive/periodicals/journal-article/psalm-2216-controversy-new-evidence-dead-sea-scrolls`; `hopkin-psalm-22-16`); see whether the page has the text or only a PDF.
- Donald W. Parry, “Temple Worship and a Possible Reference to a Prayer Circle in Psalm 24,” *BYU Studies* 32, no. 4 (1992): 57–62 (`byustudies.byu.edu/article/temple-worship-and-a-possible-reference-to-a-prayer-circle-in-psalm-24`; `parry-psalm-24`). A proposal, and given as one.
- Thomas A. Wayment, “Jesus' Use of the Psalms in Matthew,” in *Sperry Symposium Classics: The New Testament* (`rsc.byu.edu/sperry-symposium-classics-new-testament/jesus-use-psalms-matthew`; `wayment-psalms-matthew`). For 8, 22, 110, 118.
- Joseph Fielding McConkie, “Joseph Smith and the Poetic Writings,” in *The Joseph Smith Translation: The Restoration of Plain and Precious Things* (1985) (`rsc.byu.edu/joseph-smith-translation/joseph-smith-poetic-writings`; `mcconkie-poetic-writings`). “‘None That Doeth Good’” (`byustudies.byu.edu/article/none-that-doeth-good`), on the JST of Psalm 14: take author and issue from the page.
- David J. Larsen, “Psalm 1–20,” in *Old Testament Minute: Psalms* (Scripture Central, 2022) (`scripturecentral.org/archive/books/book-chapter/psalm-1-20`; `ot-minute-psalms-1-20`): a few paragraphs on verses of each of Psalms 1–20, with the temple and the king always in view. **Only this chapter was found**; addresses for 21–40 and on return 404. A writer of a later psalm may look once for its chapter by web search.
- Gerald E. Jones, “Psalms, Messianic Prophecies in,” *Encyclopedia of Mormonism* 3:1172–73, on Scripture Central's archive (`…/archive/articles/encyclopedia-entry/psalms-messianic-prophecies`; `jones-messianic-psalms`). Short.
- The FARMS Review exchange on Psalm 82 (2007): Michael Heiser's critique and David Bokovoy's response, “‘Ye Really Are Gods,’” both on Scripture Central's archive (`…/archive/periodicals/journal-article/ye-really-are-gods-response-michael-heiser-concerning-lds-use-psalm-82-and-gospel-john`). Not opened for this brief; the writer of 82 reads both before using either.
- **KnoWhys on the Psalms** (take the date from the article): #867 “Who Wrote the Psalms?” (`who-wrote-the-psalms`); #866 on Leviathan, Psalm 74:13–14; #865 on Psalm 2:7; #642 on grasping God's hand, Psalm 73:23–24; #641 on Psalm 22:16; #638 on lifting the hands in praise (Nehemiah 8:6; for 28:2, 63:4, 134:2); #437, #438, #439 on psalms in the Book of Mormon; #325 (Psalm 51 and Lehi); #313 (Psalm 46 and Zenos); #62 (Psalm 95 and Jacob); #51 (Psalm 24 and Nephi); #30 (Nephi's psalm); #655 (Psalm 2 at the Savior's baptism); #643 (Melchizedek). `knowhys.mjs psalms <n>` finds what bears on one psalm.
- In BYU Studies' bibliography and not looked for online yet: R. Scott Burton's two chapters in *Studies in Scripture* 4 (not readable, like the rest of that volume); Rodney Turner, “Why the Psalms?”, *Ensign*, Nov. 1973, and Sidney B. Sperry, “A Note on Psalm 8:4–6,” *Ensign*, Feb. 1972 (the Church's site has the *Ensign* from 1971: try the address).

**The Church's sources:**

- `bd-psalms`, `gs-psalm` (in `sources.yaml`). The Bible Dictionary's entry is four paragraphs: the Hebrew name, the count of New Testament citations (“116 have been counted” of 283), the titles, and David's authorship.
- *Scripture Helps: Old Testament*, three chapters (propose `scripture-helps-ps-1`, `scripture-helps-ps-49`, `scripture-helps-ps-102`): `…/scripture-helps-old-testament/32-psalms-1-2-3-19-33-40-46`, `…/33-psalms-49-51-61-66-69-72-77-78-85-86`, `…/34-psalms-102-103-110-116-119-127-128-135-139-146-150`. The first has “What is the book of Psalms?” and the table of messianic verses. Their endnotes lead to *The Jewish Study Bible*, Holzapfel and others' *Jehovah and the World of the Old Testament*, and Elder Holland's *For Times of Trouble* (none readable), to Hopkin and Hopkin and Hopkin's Psalm 22 (readable), and to Thomas F. Olmstead, “The Savior's Use of the Old Testament,” *Ensign*, July 2002 (readable).
- Bible Dictionary entries confirmed to exist: Selah, Hallel, “Degrees, songs of” (`bd/degrees-songs-of`), Asaph, Korah, David, Music. Not there: Maschil, Michtam. Check the page heading for any other (`STANDARDS.md` §4 rule 10).
- General conference talks on a psalm, from the talk's own page; the Joseph Smith Papers for the Prophet's use of one.
- *Old Testament Student Manual: Genesis–2 Samuel*, “The Psalms: Songs from the Heart of Israel” (`…/old-testament-student-manual-genesis-2-samuel/the-psalms-songs-from-the-heart-of-israel`; propose `ot-manual-psalms`), sections 28-1 to 28-7. **It comes after everything above**, as one voice, in no more than about a third of a chapter's notes. It is one chapter for the whole book, mostly quotations from Unger's dictionary, Eerdmans' handbook and Clarke: quote those only as the manual prints them, and prefer *Scripture Helps* where the two differ (authorship above all).

**The text and the older commentaries:**

- `oshb-wlc` (`scripts/hebrew.mjs psalms <c>:<v>`; titles and both verse numbers are handled), `biblehub-interlinear`, `bdb`, `lxx-rahlfs` (Brenton, `https://biblehub.com/sep/psalms/<c>.htm`, by the English number), `nrsvue`, `niv`, `esv`, `webster-1828`.
- Delitzsch on the Psalms, on Bible Hub at `https://biblehub.com/commentaries/kad/psalms/<c>.htm` (23 confirmed; propose `delitzsch-psalms`). **A last resort**, in no more than a third of a chapter's notes, and labelled “one reading” where the point is his alone. He numbers verses by the Hebrew.
- The saved output of `footnotes.mjs` for each psalm is in `.cache/batch/psalms/fn<n>.txt`. It gives the Church's chapter heading and footnotes; **it does not print the psalm's title** (read that from `hebrew.mjs` or the Gospel Library page).

**Keys for new sources,** so that two chapters proposing the same work choose the same key: those given above; `bd-<entry>` and `gs-<entry>`; `sc-knowhy-<number>`; for a talk, `<speaker's surname>-<one or two words of the title>`. Check `content/sources.yaml` first.

**Not readable (don't cite):**

- **The Interpreter Foundation's journal, as of 2026-10-10**: the article pages returned no text to `source.mjs` and a 404 or nothing to `curl`. Its articles on the Psalms are David J. Larsen, “Ascending into the Hill of the Lord: What the Psalms Can Tell Us About the Rituals of the First Temple” (vol. 38); Matthew B. Brown, “The Handclasp, the Temple, and the King” (vol. 42); Janet Ewell, “Seeing Psalms as the Libretti of a Holy Drama” (vol. 31); Jeff Lindsay on David and the Psalms in the Book of Mormon (vol. 29); Daniel O. McClellan, “Psalm 82 in Contemporary Latter-day Saint Tradition” (vol. 15); Stephen D. Ricks, “Psalm 105: Chiasmus, Credo, Covenant, and Temple.” Try each once when its psalm is written (the site was readable on 2026-10-07); if it fails, leave it.
- BYU ScholarsArchive PDFs; *The Jewish Study Bible*; Holzapfel, Pike and Seely, *Jehovah and the World of the Old Testament*; Elder Holland's *For Times of Trouble*; LeGrand Baker and Stephen Ricks, *Who Shall Ascend into the Hill of the Lord?* (unless a publisher's or archive copy with a text layer is found and read); *Studies in Scripture* 4; the modern commentaries on the Psalms.

## The text

`data/kjv/psalms.json` was imported on 2026-10-10 (150 psalms, 2,461 verses) and compared with the Gospel Library edition by `scripts/check-kjv.mjs psalms`. Twenty-nine verses differed; entries in `LDS_SPELLINGS` in `scripts/fetch-kjv.mjs` correct them, and the two now agree. They were of four kinds: “LORD” for the source's “Lord” at the head of eight psalms and the reverse in three verses; the source's capitals on the first words of Psalms 70, 92 and 117; punctuation (8:1; 37:4; 39:4); and spellings (“castest,” “grayheaded,” “steadfast,” “inquired,” “En-dor,” “Baal-peor,” “longsuffering,” “wits' end,” “vapour,” “twoedged”).

**The titles** above the psalms and the Hebrew letters that head the stanzas of Psalm 119 are not in that file; `scripts/fetch-psalm-titles.mjs` reads them from the Gospel Library edition into `data/kjv/psalms.titles.json` (116 titles; 22 letters), and the pages show both.

## Checks while the book is in preview

```sh
node scripts/check-chapter.mjs psalms <n>
```

It runs `fix-yaml`, the preview build into `.cache/batch/psalms/dist-NN`, `check-quotes`, `check-content` (with the proposed entries), and `check-ledger`. The working folder for a batch is `.cache/batch/psalms/` (not committed): `proposed/NNN.yaml`, `reports/NNN-writer.md`, `reports/NNN-check.md`. Chapter files are `01.yaml` to `99.yaml` and then `100.yaml` to `150.yaml`: the scripts pad a number to two digits and leave a longer one alone.

## Before the first batch

For the coordinator; none of this needs the author except where it says so.

1. **File names.** The scripts pad a chapter number to two digits (`padStart(2, '0')`), so Psalm 100 is `100.yaml`. Nothing has been run on a three-digit chapter yet: try `check-chapter psalms 100` and a listing that sorts by name (`status.mjs`, the batch scripts) before a batch reaches it.
2. **Titles in the first twelve.** Psalms 3, 8 and 22 had their title explanations moved from `setting` into notes on the title (2026-10-10, by the coordinator: the same sentences and ledger rows, moved). Psalms 23, 24, 51, 82 and 110 still open `setting` by quoting the title the page now prints above verse 1; trim that sentence when one of them is next edited.
3. **The Scriptures menu** still counts “chapters” for Psalms (`src/assets/site.js`); the pages themselves say “psalms.”
4. **The first batch is a test of this brief.** Measure it (`batch-usage.cjs`) before the second: nobody knows yet what a psalm costs.

## Decided while writing

*(`scripts/batch-decided.cjs` writes the reviewers' decisions here, by chapter.)*

Settled by the reviewers on 2026-10-10, by the standard and this brief, without the author; any of them can be overturned.

**Chapter 1**

- 1:3 "whatsoever he doeth shall prosper": cut the note "A promise other scripture tests" and its explore bullet (Ps. 73). No source read applies Jer. 12:1 or Ps. 73 to this verse; I searched Delitzsch on Psalm 1 and Psalm 73, Keil on Jeremiah 12 and Larsen and found none.
- 1:4: added one sentence answering the prosperity question from Delitzsch's comment on verse 5 (the wicked's "temporary prosperity" ends at the judgment), labelled as the same one reading, with a new ledger row I saw on the page.
- 1:4 and `christ`: cut Matt. 3:12 (John the Baptist's chaff); no source connects it to this psalm. `christ` is now two paragraphs.
- 1:6: cut Matt. 7:13-14 and 7:23 from the note and from explore. KnoWhy #287 does not mention Matthew; Delitzsch cites Matt. 7:23 here, but using him would put him in three of six notes, over the brief's limit of a third.
- 1:2: retitled "Meditating on the LORD's instruction day and night". BDB files this verse under "(soliloquize) meditate, muse", not under the sound senses, so speaking half aloud is now given as a suggestion of the word; section 1 plain changed from "murmurs over it" to "muses on it".
- 1:2: "nearly a quotation" of Josh. 1:8 reworded to "shares its words with the LORD's charge to Joshua"; added two Hebrew-text rows for "day and night".
- 1:2: cut the remark that Ps. 2:1 has the same verb (a curiosity no source applies), and cut the Larsen quotation on the man as king, which duplicated `christ`; the note keeps one sentence pointing to Ps. 2:2.
- 1:1 structure note: "not on rhyme or metre" became "not on rhyme"; cut "which is why the King James Version still reads as poetry"; cut "the verbs slow down" and "settings become more fixed" (the writer's own), leaving the climax of the three names as Delitzsch's one reading.
- 1:1 structure note: kept verse 6 as an example of antithetic parallelism. The manual supplies the definition, and the verse's "but" contrast is plain in the text.
- 1:3 tree: "deliberately", "running", "not luck or native vigor", "not every month" and "dry months" cut; the transplanted-tree and canal senses are now conditional ("if those senses hold here").
- 1:4: "the Old Testament mentions it only..." narrowed to the Hebrew word used here (BDB's entry is for that one word); cut "the grain stays" and the line on the psalm's proportions, which repeated the thread.
- 1:1 Beatitudes note and `christ`: the Skinner quotation is printed once, in the note; `christ` paraphrases and points to it. Cut the "framed by two sayings" sentence, which repeated the setting.
- 1:6: cut the sentences on the LORD acting and the way perishing by itself, which repeated the thread.
- Explore: "shrub" changed to the King James word "heath"; the first bullet no longer repeats the "Blessed" frame.
- Left as they were: "contains none [no praise]", "no title", "three Hebrew words" (confirmed with hebrew.mjs psalms 1:1 and 1:4), and plain-words "advice" and "path", which keep the King James sense.

**Chapter 2**

- 2:12, thread, christ, tagline: the chapter said the refuge of the last line is 'in the Son'. No row supported that, and Delitzsch (the one commentary cited) reads 'him' as Jehovah ('they hide themselves in Jahve as their refuge'). All four places now quote the verse's own words and do not say who 'him' is.
- 2:4: the claim that Psalms 37:13 and 59:8 'support' the commentator's reading, and 'in each the laughter belongs to the One who already sees the end', were the writer's own. The two psalms stay as the image's other occurrences, with only 37:13's own stated reason. Also cut: 'not amusement at anyone's ruin', 'the conference is absurd', 'the revolt is against an accomplished fact', and the 'installed' rendering with its lexicon row. 'One commentator glosses' is reworded.
- 2:7 (Israel's king): 'The decree goes back to the covenant with David' is now 'one of those tied to the Lord's covenant with David', with a new row from Scripture Helps endnote 15. Cut: 'they make him king', the unsourced inference about 'this day', and the closing paragraph ('the king's answer to the revolt').
- 2:7 (New Testament): cut the Romans 1:4 sentence and its row (no source ties it to the psalm) and 'the two royal psalms together name Him king and priest'. 'Was heard by early readers as this psalm' is now 'connected with this psalm by at least some early readers', matching the KnoWhy. 'In fact and in office both' is reworded to the KnoWhy's own two senses.
- 2:9: 'the New Testament follows the gentler one' is now 'Revelation quotes the line in the second'. Cut: 'the Savior's own reading of the psalm', 'the iron rod in the Son's hand is first a shepherd's', and the 1 Nephi 11:25 link with its row (no source applies it to this verse). The Joseph Smith Translation of Revelation 2:27 is now said to have 'the word of God where the rod of iron stood', not to say 'what the rod is'.
- 2:12: cut the closing sentence that harmonised the three readings. 'One standard lexicon' and 'a recent translation' are reworded; the New Revised Standard Version is named as a translation.
- 2:1 (first Saints): cut the superlative 'earliest recorded use', the 'vain thing in the fullest sense' inference and the 'do not ask for the rod of iron' contrast. The king/ruler mapping is reworded to what Acts 4:27 lists, and Acts 4:21-24 is referenced for the council's threat. The note stays although the footnote and Scripture Helps give Acts 4:25-27, because it goes on to the Greek wording and Acts 4:28-29.
- 2:1 (meditate): 'such echoes are part of why' the two psalms are read together was the writer's link. The shared words and the tradition of reading the psalms together are now stated separately.
- 2:2: 'the title belonged to the office, whoever held it' is now 'Saul bore the title as well'. The 'the lexicon files...' wording is reworded so the source is not announced.
- Thread: dropped 'each scene has its own speaker' and 'the Why is astonishment'. The Blessed/Blessed frame with Psalm 1 now rests on a new row from Scripture Helps endnote 7.
- Setting: the royal-psalm definition was quoted both there and in 2:2; the setting now points to the note on 2:2. The Scripture Helps sentence on Psalms 1-2 as an introduction is paraphrased (Psalm 1 carries it). Revelation 19:15 in the list of New Testament readings is replaced by Revelation 2:26-27, which the source lists.
- Christ: the Scripture Helps quotation on messianic psalms is paraphrased, since other psalms will want the same sentence.
- Explore: cut 'The rod and the word' (Isaiah 11:4 and D&C 19:15 are already the Church's footnote 9a).
- Left as is, verse 6: the paraphrase keeps 'installed' (lexicon row), which agrees with the King James 'set'. The footnote's 'HEB anointed' is beside the verse and is not repeated.
- Left as is, 2:9: the break/shepherd question stays as a Language note. It rests on the Hebrew, the Greek and Revelation's own wording, and contradicts no Church source.
- Left as is: the Larsen chapter (Old Testament Minute) is quoted in the setting and three notes but is the main idea of only one (the royal psalm, 2:2). Delitzsch stays in 2 of 8 notes, each as one reading.

**Chapter 3**

- Setting: 'The psalm is a lament' became 'The psalm opens in lament', which is what the source says (verses 1-2).
- Setting: the general claim that a title may name an occasion was replaced by what the Bible Dictionary says (titles mainly say how a psalm was sung) and what this title itself does; the mizmor sentence was cut as a side detail.
- Setting: added one sentence, sourced to Delitzsch, that the psalm's leading features have been matched to the story (mockery, 2 Sam. 16:7-8; danger by night, 17:1; multitudes, 15:13 and 17:11). This carries the story links the notes had asserted on their own.
- 3:1: cut 'The psalm's numbers are the story's numbers' and the twenty thousand of 2 Sam. 18:7 (the writer's joining); the closing paragraph now says only what verse 6 says.
- 3:2 (taunt): cut the Shimei paragraph, 'does not argue that David deserves rescue', and the Jonah comparison. The note is now the yeshuah word link alone; Jonah 2:9 stays in explore and parallels.
- 3:2 (Selah): the pause reading (the newer Church reading, and the largest group per Hopkin and Hopkin) now comes first, with the Bible Dictionary's 'strike up' as the other explanation. Cut the mizmor tie and 'It does not come after verse 6', which raised a question the note did not answer.
- 3:3: the covered head beside the lifted head, and 'my glory' beside the lost kingdom, are Delitzsch's and are now given as 'one older reading' with two ledger rows. Cut 'the shield is there first'.
- 3:4: cut 'comes from the psalm before', 'praying toward', and 'the distance made no difference'. The note now says the hill means the temple (Larsen), that Ps. 2:6 has the same phrase and names Zion, and what 2 Sam. 6:17 and 15:25 say.
- 3:5: retitled 'Sleeping and waking' and rebuilt on Larsen (trust lets him sleep; the morning prayer; Psalm 4). The Ahithophel tie was cut from the note because 2 Sam. 17:16-22 has David crossing the Jordan that night; the 'danger by night' link survives only as Delitzsch's, in the setting.
- 3:7 (Arise): retitled 'The call Moses made'. Cut 'David takes up his words', 'whenever', and the sentence about the ark sent back. The note rests on the identical Hebrew of Num. 10:35 and this verse. Delitzsch also makes this link ('taken from the mouth of Moses') but is not cited here, to hold the brief's limit of a third of the notes.
- 3:7 (cheek): cut the 'Deal gently' link to 2 Sam. 18:5; 'it is not a wish' became a statement of what the verse asks for.
- 3:8: retitled 'A blessing on thy people'. That the people are Absalom's followers is now 'one reading', not fact; cut the comparison with David's grief in 2 Sam. 18:33.
- christ: 'says what David said and adds a name to it' became 'The same confession is made in John's vision', which is as far as the source goes.
- Delitzsch is now cited in 3 of 9 notes (3:3, 3:7 cheek, 3:8), plus the setting and christ. That is the brief's limit; each use is labelled a reading.

**Chapter 8**

- setting: cut 'found only in Chronicles and in psalm titles' for the verb behind 'chief Musician'; the same lexicon entry lists Habakkuk 3:19.
- setting: 'so the scene is night' softened to 'which suggests a night sky'; 'Genesis 1 turned into words addressed to God' and 'uses most pointedly' cut as the writer's own.
- setting and note 8:6: Paul 'uses the words of' verse 6 (1 Cor. 15:27; Eph. 1:22) rather than 'quoted' it, since the rows show shared wording only.
- thread: 'the gift has so far been fully received by one Man only' replaced with what Hebrews 2:8-9 says (not yet all things under man; Jesus crowned).
- 8:1: cut 'addressed by a congregation', 'sets the direction of the whole psalm' and 'excellent is stronger than it now sounds' (no source); 'the same two words' in Ps. 110:1 corrected to 'the name and the title', since 110:1 has 'my Lord', not 'our Lord'.
- 8:2: cut 'the two wordings are closer than they look' and the closing join to the rest of the psalm; the commentary read says the Greek sense is not the Hebrew word's own. The 'feeble instruments' reading stays as 'one reading'.
- 8:4: 'is not a title' reworded to what the parallelism and the translation show (a human being); 'a later psalm' became 'another psalm' (no psalm is dated without a source); 'the tone is wonder', 'for him the attention is a weight' and 'only Psalm 8 answers with a crown' reduced to what the three texts plainly say. The Job and Psalm 144 comparison stays although footnote 4a lists both, because the note quotes and contrasts them rather than listing them.
- 8:5: the senses of Elohim put in the lexicon's order (the verse is in its second group, not the last) and the lexicon no longer announced; the lexicon's placement is given as what some interpreters have held, with 'a little lower than God' stated as the more accurate translation following Scripture Helps, not as an equal alternative.
- 8:5: the 'hesitancy among translators' wording now refers to the King James and other translations, as the endnote does, not to the Greek translators; 'the Church's study help says' and 'The Church's help judges' removed; 'Latter-day Saints have no reason to soften the line' cut; 'the usual word for God' became 'a name for God'.
- 8:5: kept although footnote 5a and Scripture Helps give the Elohim point, because the note goes further (the word's range, the Greek, the route of 'angels' into the King James through Hebrews and the Latin). The two works behind Scripture Helps endnote 19 are unreadable and uncited.
- 8:6 (Genesis): 'sixth day' cut (no row); 'He is repeating what God said', 'verse 5's nearness stands where Genesis has the image and likeness' and 'things that remain God's' cut as the writer's joins; the note now rests on the comparison its source makes with Genesis 1:26.
- 8:6 (Christ) and christ: Hebrews 2:10 corrected so God, not Jesus, is the one bringing many sons to glory, with the verse quoted; 'least of all death', 'outline of the Savior's descent and exaltation', 'the crown of verse 5 is reached through Him' and 'dominion over sheep and oxen has become' cut or reduced; the pointer to Ps. 110:1 reworded to what that verse says (footstool).
- christ: 'points to Jesus Christ twice in the New Testament' reworded, since the chapter itself gives four passages.
- explore: cut 'Moses under the same sky' (Moses 1), a connection no source read applies to this psalm, and its ledger row; cut 'The question in other mouths', which repeated the note on 8:4 and parallels.
- Left as is: the one-outside-source rule for Larsen's Old Testament Minute. It is cited in four notes but is the main idea of none (each rests chiefly on the Hebrew, the lexicon, Genesis or Hebrews), and it is a Scripture Central work.
- Left as is: no Latter-day Prophets note. The writer found only President Nelson quoting verses 3-5 without comment; a note would have been a quotation of a quotation.

**Chapter 22**

- Setting, 22:16, christ: the Church's chapter heading was quoted in three places; it is now quoted once, in setting (STANDARDS 1, quote the Church's material sparingly).
- Setting: trimmed the New Testament paragraph, which christ repeats, and the aside that the site does not show titles; setting is now inside 150-250 words.
- Thread: 'the same word comes twice more' reworded to 'twice more he pleads against that distance', since the Hebrew has an adjective in verse 1 and a verb in verses 11 and 19; the closing line now uses verse 1's own words ('so far from helping').
- 22:1 The shape of a lament: cut 'the darkest in the psalm', the writer's own judgment.
- 22:3: cut the two closing sentences on what the complaint of verse 1 'is', a reading no source makes.
- 22:8: cut 'without marking them as a quotation' (no row).
- 22:12: cut 'The prayer undoes the ring one animal at a time...' (the writer's reading); the reversed order of the animals stays as an observation of the text. 'The lexicon marks' reworded so no source is announced.
- 22:15: the thirst reading of the potsherd is now given as a reading ('has been read as'), since it is Hoskisson's as Hopkin prints it; cut 'the complaint of verse 1 again, at its lowest point' and the unsourced pointer to Ps. 69:21.
- 22:16: Aquila and Symmachus are no longer set 'against' the alteration claim; the note now says, from the article, that they worked after the dispute began and 'were likely influenced by it', yet also have a plural verb (new ledger row).
- 22:16: 'The Gospels quote other lines and never this one' replaced by 'The Gospels tell that He was crucified (Luke 23:33); they do not specifically mention the piercing... though this was a typical mode of crucifixion', so the note does not stand against the Scripture Helps pairing of 22:16 with Luke 23:33.
- 22:16: 'the conclusion drawn in the Latter-day Saint studies' kept, since the brief asks for the conclusion to be given as theirs; 'The standard lexicon calls' reworded.
- 22:18: cut 'the man is not yet dead, and his clothes are already being shared out' (inference of the writer's).
- 22:21: the paragraph on Christ now says only what Hopkin says (the praise follows His death); cut 'the answer is not a rescue from dying' and the Hebrews 5:7 comparison, which rested on a 'perhaps' Delitzsch wrote of another phrase. Two rows removed, lds-scriptures dropped from the note's sources.
- 22:22: 'a gathering the study finds described...' reworded so no source is announced; it stays one reading.
- 22:26: the lead now gives the thank-offering meal as a reading ('has been read as'), since it rests on Delitzsch alone and no Church or BYU source was found; cut the Beatitudes paragraph (a cross-reference, and one more idea of Hopkin's) and the unsourced 'Neither the psalm nor the New Testament makes that connection'; the sacrament reading is kept in one sentence as a reading.
- 22:27: opening sentence no longer says one man's deliverance 'becomes the reason the whole world worships'; 'hard in Hebrew' and 'Either way the dead are included' replaced by what the two translations themselves show; Elder McConkie's words are tied to verses 27-28, which is what he was speaking of.
- 22:31: cut the sentence joining verse 1 to the last word (said once, in thread).
- Christ: cut 'The shape of the psalm matters as much as its details' and the sentence that Isaiah's servant song 'moves the same way', a connection no row states; Isaiah 53 remains in explore and in the notes on 22:6 and 22:31.
- Explore: 'which an older commentary calls' reworded to 'which has been called'.
- Hopkin's 2013 article is in the sources of 13 of 15 notes, against the rule of thumb in STANDARDS 4 rule 11. Left: the brief names it as the Latter-day Saint study of the psalm, and each note starts from the text or the New Testament. One of his side points (the Beatitudes) was cut.
- Prayer first, fulfilment after, with 'need not be mutually exclusive': kept as the brief proposes, though Elder McConkie (as Hopkin prints him) reads verse 1 as the Holy Ghost revealing the words beforehand; the chapter does not deny that reading.
- Gospel Library overlap (finding 4): notes on 22:1, 22:6, 22:8, 22:12, 22:18 and 22:22 start where a footnote or the Scripture Helps table does and go further (the first line as title, the shared Hebrew verb, the Greek vocabulary, John's two acts, the Greek 'church'); kept.
- Scripture Helps endnotes: Hopkin 2013 and Hopkin and Hopkin are cited; Olmstead's Ensign article is readable but answers no question the chapter leaves open, so it was not added.
- Proposed entry hopkin-psalm-22: end page 151 could not be seen on the page, so pub now says 'beginning on page 117'. hopkin-psalm-22-16 keeps the archive page as its URL; the ledger rows cite the PDF read.

**Chapter 23**

- 23:1 'Shepherd was a king's title': cut the unsourced 'two things are unusual' paragraph and the mapping of provision and protection onto verses 1-3 and 4; replaced with 2 Sam. 5:2, which the lexicon row already cites for the shepherding verb used of David, so the king-as-sheep point now rests on a source. The Ps. 80:1 contrast is gone (it pulled against Jacob in the setting).
- 23:1 'Want means lack': dropped 'the older English' (no row) and the sentence joining Deut. 2:7 to the psalm's theme; Deut. 2:7 stays as an example of the same verb.
- 23:2 'Waters of rest': cut 'the stillness belongs to the one who drinks' (no source).
- 23:3 'Brought back to life': cut the sentence about a sheep revived or fetched back (no source).
- 23:3 'Right tracks': replaced the writer's Ezek. 20:9 explanation of 'for his name's sake' with Delitzsch's, labelled 'one reading' and given a new ledger row; reworded the Hilton sentence so the caution is his general one about all allusions.
- 23:4 'Death's shadow': restored the lexicon's hedges ('probably' a compound; the Greek 'usually'; 'most modern' scholars); no longer says the King James follows the Greek; cut 'the psalm does not exclude the last valley' (beyond both Scripture Helps and the lexicon) and the paragraph on walking 'through' (the Hebrew has 'in').
- 23:4 'From he to thou at the center': kept the 26/3/26 word count as an observation of the Hebrew text (the checker confirmed it) with its hedge; cut the interpretive close that 'everything on either side depends on this'.
- 23:5 'The shepherd becomes a host': cut 'here they have faces' (no source); the Absalom setting stays as 'one older reading', hedged as the brief requires.
- 23:5 'A guest's anointing': cut the Luke 7:46 paragraph (a connection no source makes for this verse) and the Delitzsch banquet phrase; 'the Psalms keep it for the king' became 'the verb the Psalms use of the king'. Delitzsch is removed from this note so he stays in 4 of 12 notes, at the brief's limit of a third.
- 23:6 'Pursued by goodness': the hesed gloss 'loyal covenant love' (no row) is removed, leaving the pointer to Ps. 51:1; 'the Psalms' word for what enemies do' softened to 'elsewhere in the Psalms'.
- 23:6 'Dwell or return': cut the link of the homecoming to verse 3 (not Delitzsch's) and the claim that 'later scripture' reaches as far as 'for ever'.
- christ: cut the claim that Revelation 7 gathers the psalm's images (want, temple, house); kept Rev. 7:17 for the Lamb as shepherd leading to water, which the cross-reference row ties to verse 2; trimmed the overlap with the note on verse 1 to a pointer.
- thread: the three-part pattern is now said of 'many' laments, as the source says, and 'this psalm is only that last part' became a plain observation that it has no complaint or request.
- explore: the hymn line no longer claims 'nearly line by line'; it says the hymn puts the psalm into verse from its first line to the valley, with a second ledger row for the valley line.
- No rod-and-staff note: Scripture Helps answers it beside the verse and the brief says not to repeat it; left as the writer decided.

**Chapter 24**

- 24:6 'thy face, O Jacob': the Church footnote's 'even Jacob' is given first as the edition's reading; the Greek and Syriac 'God of Jacob' follows as the textual variant most translators accept, not as an equal rival heading. Cut 'the true Israel', 'the psalmist turns mid-sentence', 'the oldest translations', and the unsourced remark that 'O' is the King James translators' addition.
- 24:5 'righteousness from the God of his salvation': the note no longer carries Elder Bednar (its substance was the passage Scripture Helps prints at 24:3-4); it is now a short Language note on the Greek 'mercy' and the NRSVUE 'vindication'. Bednar is said once, in `christ`, paraphrased, with only 'before Him who is pure' and 'freely spilled His precious blood for us' quoted. James 4:8 cut (no source or footnote ties it to the psalm).
- christ: cut 1 Cor. 2:8 'Lord of glory' and Alma 5:50 as the chapter's own joins (Alma 5:50 is only a Church footnote cross-reference at 8b); Delitzsch's 'hymn in honour of the Lord who should come into His temple' is now 'on one reading', not what 'has long been heard'; 'only by the Atonement' reworded to what the talk says.
- 24:6 Matt. 5:8 note: 'is this psalm in one sentence' became 'rests on this psalm' (Skinner's wording); the D&C 97:16 paragraph cut from the note since no source read ties it to Psalm 24. D&C 97:16 stays in `explore` and `parallels` as a plain pointer, its ledger row re-filed under `parallels`.
- 24:10 LORD of hosts: cut 'That is why... has lasted' (causal claims no row states) and the Ex. 15:3 link (Delitzsch makes it, but citing him in a fifth note would pass the brief's one-third limit); the ark reading now points back to the background, and the Kraus phrase is given as said of the Psalms generally.
- 24:3 hill note: first paragraph shortened so it does not repeat Scripture Helps on har/mountain and the holy place; the note's own work (David's tent, why go up, the temple in the Psalms) is kept as the batch's anchor. 'Verse 6 names the same hope' reduced to the plain observation that verse 6 also speaks of seeking His face.
- 24:3 dialogue note: cut 'had to be answered by someone, aloud, each time' (the chapter's inference), the remark about the parentheses, and Ps. 118:20 (no source).
- 24:1: cut the 'keeper of a local shrine' contrast and D&C 104:14 (the chapter's own comparison); Paul's use in 1 Cor. 10:25-26 kept because it says what the footnote's bare reference does not.
- 24:4 hands and heart: 'what lies behind them' reworded to deeds and thoughts as Skinner has it; closing paragraph ('names no lineage, office, or offering') cut as the chapter's reading.
- 24:7 gates: closing paragraph comparing the two halves ('Nothing is asked of the King but His name') cut as the chapter's reading.
- 24:9 JST: cut 'so the seekers of the first half become the ones addressed' and the closing paragraph linking D&C 88:95 to verse 6; kept the plain observation that 'generation' and 'Jacob' are words of verse 6, and LeFevre's dating and D&C 88:95 comparison, which is what goes beyond the footnote.
- thread: cut 'What a person lifts his soul toward decides what he carries away, and in the end even the doors must rise' (the writer's own summary, which the writer flagged); the textual fact that nasa lies behind 'lifted up', 'receive' and 'lift up' stays, and the verse 5 note keeps its one-line restatement of verses 4-5.
- setting and explore: 'Its nearest relative is Psalm 15' became 'Psalm 15 opens with nearly the same question'; the characterisation of Psalm 15's answer cut from explore. The Sinai and Peniel items stay in explore as scripture threads that claim nothing about the psalm's meaning.
- Scripture Helps endnote 29 (Skinner, 'Israel's Ancient Psalms') was opened: it makes the same Matt. 5:8 argument as Skinner's Sperry essay the chapter already cites, so it was not added.

**Chapter 51**

- setting: 'Lehi prays the words of verse 1' reworded to 'Lehi's prayer in his dream is very close to verse 1' (KnoWhy #325 says 'very similar').
- 51:1: the judgment that Lehi or Nephi had the psalm in mind is now given as a reading ('has been taken to show'), not as fact; 'David had a particular reason to reach for the word' reworded to the plain observation that the word had been spoken to David in 2 Sam. 7:15.
- 51:2: 'wipe a written record away' replaced by BDB's own 'obliterate from the memory'; 'worked over like stained cloth' cut (BDB marks the psalm's use as figurative).
- 51:4: 'answering Nathan in Nathan's own words' and 'joins the two' cut; the shared Hebrew words with 2 Sam. 12:9, 13 stay as an observation from the text.
- 51:7: cut 'puts David where the leper stood', 'more than touch the dead', 'no one stands between' and the Isa. 1:18 pairing (no source applies them here); the note now says the request is made in the language of those rites, which BDB's entry supports.
- 51:10: cut 'The order in this psalm is the order there' (the psalm asks for the clean heart before the broken one is offered, and the KnoWhy relays a reading about 'our' heart); sc-knowhy-198 removed from this note and its row deleted; the Ezek. 36:26 bullet in explore cut as a duplicate.
- 51:11: retitled 'The Spirit that came on David and departed from Saul' and opened as 'One reading', since the Saul connection is Delitzsch's alone; the 2 Sam. 7:15 link (the writer's, and a repeat of 51:1) cut; 'in the days of Moses' cut (row covers Isa. 63:10 only).
- 51:12: retitled 'A willing spirit'; the distribution of the three spirits is now worded 'Read so'.
- 51:14: the Bible Dictionary sentence was printed under 'Latter-day revelation is more exact' and repeated in the close; now D&C 132:39 is given as the revelation and the Church's teaching paraphrased once. Joseph Smith's words are marked as Wilford Woodruff's record. Num. 35:31 cut (the writer's own cross-reference). The closing summary reduced to 'Psalm 51 itself announces no pardon.'
- 51:14: Scripture Helps ('could be forgiven ... except for the murder of Uriah') and the Bible Dictionary ('still unforgiven') are not set against each other; the note follows the wording they share, as the brief's statement on David directs.
- 51:16: the KnoWhy #27 quotation is now attached to what it speaks of (the Psalms on the broken heart), not to 1 Sam. 15:22 and Ps. 50:9 as 'voices'.
- 51:17: cut 'What God has crushed is what David brings', 'The man who despised the Lord asks that his broken heart not be despised', and 'which would explain how Lehi's family knew'; the shared verbs with verse 8 and 2 Sam. 12:10 stay as observations.
- 51:18: kept on Delitzsch alone, since no Church, BYU or Scripture Central source read answers why the psalm ends with Jerusalem's walls; 'explained in two ways' reworded to an open question, 'The commentator who grants...' (an announced source) rewritten, and the writer's closing reading replaced by Delitzsch's sentence, whose row moved here from 51:16. Delitzsch stays in 3 of 11 notes.
- christ: cut 'God did not desire David's bullock because the altar looked toward another sacrifice', 'Lehi gives the reason a broken heart is enough', the David/Servant contrast, and the last paragraph on Acts 2:31 (it joined Peter's words to David's own promise and repeated note 51:14); the repeated 3 Ne. 9:20 quotation shortened to 'unto me'.
- christ, Isa. 53:5: 'the same word, in a slightly different spelling' changed to 'a closely related form' (BDB lists two entries, H1794 and H1792; Scripture Helps calls them the same word).
- thread: 'every line that is not confession is a request' and 'He claims nothing, not even an offering' corrected to what the verses say (verses 13-17 are vow and statement).
- 51:5 has no note, as the brief directs (Scripture Helps answers it beside the verse).
- Left as written: the Acts 3:19 paragraph in christ (footnote 1c gives the reference; the paragraph adds 'converted' and the point to Christ); section plain 'brought forth in iniquity' (does not depart from the King James sense).

**Chapter 82**

- 82:6 (gods): restructured so the Church's reading (Scripture Helps: the council scene, with appointed judges addressed) comes first and the heavenly-beings and 'both' readings are given as Latter-day Saint scholars' arguments, not equals; retitled 'Who the gods of the psalm are'; 'the fullest Latter-day Saint study' reworded to 'one Latter-day Saint study' (no source ranks it).
- 82:6 (children of the Most High): cut the contrast with D&C 76:53, 59-60 and the closing 'warning with the dignity' paragraph (the chapter's own connection); cut 'Usually they cite the verse beside Acts 17:29' (no row); Scripture Helps sentence paraphrased, not quoted.
- 82:6 (the Savior's defense): cut 'in the temple', 'which fits people who received a divine commission' and 'rests everything on a single word'; the second reading reworded to what Frederick says (rulers with granted authority; an argument from scripture's use of the title), not 'finds the argument sound as it stands'.
- 82:3: retitled 'Defend is the verb judge'; cut 'Four words' (the Hebrew has five), Deut. 10:18, Deut. 1:17 and 2 Chr. 19:6 (no source applies them to the verse); kept Deut. 10:17 only as the same Hebrew idiom, which I confirmed with hebrew.mjs in both verses.
- 82:5: Ps. 11:3 kept as 'the same picture' (Delitzsch's cross-reference), the gloss 'a society whose law has failed' and the closing flourish cut.
- 82:7: 'like Adam' now rests on Pritchett's article (read this session, new ledger row) with the KnoWhy; Bokovoy's 'often appear to receive' and his condition restored; Abr. 3:27-28 and Moses 4:4 dropped (only Moses 4:3 is on his page); one unsourced aphorism cut.
- 82:8: Deut. 32:8-9 kept as shared words (verb and title confirmed in the Hebrew of both verses) and the variant as 'one reading'; cut 'over the nations', the 'reversal of the arrangement' sentence, 'take up what is already His', and the Ps. 2:8 paragraph (it stays in christ).
- 82:1: Scripture Helps quotation paraphrased; cut 'In those scenes the council deliberates' and 'The charge in the psalm is the same' (the chapter's own comparison with Isaiah 3); 'scholars who have written on the psalm' corrected to 'Latter-day Saint scholars'.
- setting and thread: the chapter heading quotation removed (Gospel Library, with its ledger row); two Scripture Helps quotations paraphrased; 'unlike almost every other psalm', 'the one Judge who cannot be corrupted' and 'those who held the nations in trust have lost them' cut or reworded as unsourced.
- sections: the five 'plain' paraphrases reworded, since they followed a modern translation nearly word for word.
- christ and explore: cut Rev. 11:15 and the Luke 1:32 'died as a man and rose' typology (the writer's own); Ps. 2:8 kept with a pointer to Ps. 2:2; Rom. 8:16-17 line in explore reworded to what the verses say. The Peterson essay stays named in explore as the one outside pointer.
- Left as they are: the speaker of verse 8 as 'the psalmist' (the verse addresses God, so it is the text's own); 'the standard lexicon' phrasing; Kearon's quotation; the Joseph Smith sermon note.

**Chapter 110**

- 110:1 (LORD and Lord): replaced the chapter's own inference that "the one who enthrones is the Father" (from Acts 2:36 and D&C 76:20) with Heb. 1:13 and 1:5, which quote this verse as God's word to His Son; cut "it fits this verse" and "The psalm does not sort the Godhead by its names"; D&C 76:20 row removed.
- 110:1 (The question no one could answer): replaced the outside reading ("better understood not as Son of David but as Son of God", given as "one reading") with the Church's statement in Scripture Helps: New Testament that Christ was "more than just the son of David; He was also the Son of God"; new key scripture-helps-nt-matt-21.
- 110:1 (right hand, footstool, until): "each of its three parts is taken up there" reworded, since the third rests on D&C 49:6 and not the New Testament; cut the claim that verse 2 describes the same interval as D&C 49:6. The KnoWhy #307 quotation is left as it is.
- 110:2: cut "something carried" and "which grows", which are not on the lexicon page; "the companion psalm" became "Psalm 2".
- 110:3 (volunteers): "the word for power is also the word for a host" reworded to the rendering the lexicon row prints ("in the day of thy host"); cut Judg. 5:2 and the closing line tying the sanctuary word to the priesthood of verse 4, both the chapter's own connections.
- 110:3 (dew): cut the comparison of the Greek reading with Ps. 2:7, which no source makes.
- 110:4 (Hebrews): "builds its account on this one verse" (and the same claim in `setting`) reworded to "quotes this verse again and again", since Hebrews also argues from Genesis 14 and Psalm 2:7.
- 110:4 (king and priest): cut Zech. 6:13 (own connection) and the closing sentence joining D&C 107:3, Hebrews and the psalm; "explains how a son of Judah could hold such a priesthood" reworded to what the manual says (not by descent); the second quotation of Heb. 7:14 in the chapter became a paraphrase.
- 110:5: cut Rev. 19:15 (the commentary cites Rev. 19:17 and 18:21, not that verse); "can only be the king" softened to "is read as the king".
- 110:7: added that a modern translation prints "heads" in verse 6 (new NRSVUE row, confirmed by check-ledger); trimmed the closing summary to what the text shows.
